.
But it is easy to compute the scalar: by applying the explicit formula (
80
) to the element
\prod_{j}(1+T_{j})^{p-1}\underline{Z}^{-\underline{1}}

we obtain

\mu\circ a^{-1}=N_{\mathbb{F}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\overline{a})\mu\quad\forall\ a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}.

Suppose
\mu\in\Hom^{\cont}_{\mathbb{F}}(A,\mathbb{F})
is nonzero such that
\mu\circ\psi=(-1)^{f-1}\mu
and
D
is an étale
(\varphi,\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
-module over
A
. Then composition with
\mu
induces an
A
-linear map

\mu_{*}:\Hom_{A}(D,A)\to\Hom^{\cont}_{\mathbb{F}}(D,\mathbb{F}).

(84)

Recall from Lemma
3.2.2
that
\Hom_{A}(D,A)
is naturally an étale
(\varphi,\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
-module. The following
lemma will allow us to calculate this structure on the level of
\Hom^{\cont}_{\mathbb{F}}(D,\mathbb{F})
.

Lemma 3.3.5
.

(i)

The map
\mu_{*}
in (
84
) is injective.

(ii)

We have
\mu_{*}(\varphi(h))=(-1)^{f-1}\mu_{*}(h)\circ\psi
.

(iii)

We have
\mu_{*}(a(h))=N_{\mathbb{F}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\overline{a})^{-1}\mu_{*}(h)\circ a^{-1}
for
a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
.

Proof.

Part (iii) follows immediately from Remark
3.3.4
.

For (i) we can reduce to
D=A
by using that
D
is finite projective. Observe then that the kernel of
\mu_{*}

is an
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
-stable ideal of
A
by (iii); but by
[
BHH+
, Cor. 3.1.1.7]
it is zero, as it cannot be all of
A
.
(Alternatively part (i) also follows from the explicit
formula for
\mu
above.)

Part (ii) follows from the explicit formula (
71
) for
\varphi
on
\Hom_{A}(D,A)
as well as the two conditions
at the beginning of the proof of Proposition
3.3.1
.
∎

We make part (ii) more explicit. Suppose that
h\in\Hom_{\mathbb{F}}^{\cont}(D_{A}(\pi),\mathbb{F})
corresponds to a sequence
(x_{k})_{k\geq 0}

as in Proposition
3.2.3
. Then
(-1)^{f-1}h\circ\psi
corresponds to a sequence
(x^{\prime}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
determined by
the relation

x^{\prime}_{pk}=(-1)^{f-1}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)x_{k},

(85)

since
\psi\circ\underline{Y}^{-\underline{pk}}=\underline{Y}^{-\underline{k}}\circ\psi
on
D_{A}(\pi)
.

Lemma 3.3.6
.

Suppose that
D
is a finite projective
A
-module.
Then the image of
\mu_{*}:\Hom_{A}(D,A)\hookrightarrow\Hom^{\cont}_{\mathbb{F}}(D,\mathbb{F})
consists precisely of all continuous
\mathbb{F}
-linear maps
h:D\to\mathbb{F}
such that
for all
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and all
x\in D
the set
X^{\prime}_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\{\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}:h(\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}}x)\neq 0,\|\underline{i}\|=M\}
is finite.

Equivalently, the image of
\mu_{*}:\Hom_{A}(D,A)\hookrightarrow\Hom^{\cont}_{\mathbb{F}}(D,\mathbb{F})
consists precisely of all continuous
\mathbb{F}
-linear maps
h:D\to\mathbb{F}
such that
for all
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and all
x\in D
the set
X_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\{\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}:h(\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}}x)\neq 0,\|\underline{i}\|=M\}
is finite.

Proof.

For the first part it is easy to reduce to the case where
D=A
, using the compatibility of
\mu_{*}
with direct sums

D=D_{1}\oplus D_{2}
. If
h=\mu_{*}(a)
for some
a\in A
and
x\in A
, then we write

ax\prod_{j}(1+T_{j})^{-1}=\sum_{\underline{i}}\lambda_{\underline{i}}\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}}
for
\lambda_{\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{F}
. Then

h(\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}}x)=\lambda_{-\underline{i}-\underline{1}}
(by the explicit formula for
\mu_{*}
in §
3.3
), so

h(\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}}x)\neq 0
can only happen for finitely many
\underline{i}
of any fixed degree
\|\underline{i}\|=M
.
Conversely, if
h:A\to\mathbb{F}
is continuous such that for all
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
the set

\{\underline{i}:h(\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}})\neq 0,\|\underline{i}\|=M\}
is finite, then by continuity of
h
and the finiteness assumption it
follows that
a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}(\prod_{j}(1+T_{j}))\sum_{\underline{i}}h(\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}})\underline{Z}^{-\underline{i}-\underline{1}}\in A
, and by the explicit formula
for
\mu_{*}
we have
\mu_{*}(a)=h
.

To justify the second part, recall that
Y_{j}
,
Z_{j}\in\mathbb{F}\llbracket N_{0}\rrbracket
with
\gr(Y_{j})=\gr(Z_{j})
, so

Z_{j}=Y_{j}\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}F_{d,j}
, where
Y_{j}F_{d,j}
is a homogeneous polynomial in
Y_{0},\dots,Y_{f-1}
of degree
d+1

and
F_{0,j}=1
.
Define the subring

A_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\Big\{\sum_{d=0}^{\infty}\frac{F_{d}}{\underline{Y}^{\underline{d}}}:\text{$F_{d}$ a homog.\ poly.\ in $Y_{0},\dots,Y_{f-1}$ of degree $d(f+1)$}\Big\}

of
A
with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{0}
defined by the condition
F_{0}=0
. The above observation then implies that

Z_{j}\in Y_{j}(1+\mathfrak{m}_{0})
for any
j
, hence

\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}}\in\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}}(1+\mathfrak{m}_{0})\quad\forall\ \underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}.

(86)

Also note that

A_{0}=\bigg\{\sum_{\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f};\ k_{j}\geq-\|\underline{k}\|\;\forall j}\lambda_{\underline{k}}\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}:\lambda_{\underline{k}}\in\mathbb{F}\bigg\}

(87)

and that the condition
k_{j}\geq-\|\underline{k}\|
for all
j
implies
k_{j}\leq f\|\underline{k}\|
for all
j
(and
\|\underline{k}\|\geq 0
),
so that there are only finitely many terms of any fixed degree.

Fix now
x\in D
and suppose that the set
X_{N}=\{\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}:h(\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}}x)\neq 0,\|\underline{i}\|=N\}
is finite for any
N\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By continuity of
h
we know that
h(\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}}x)=0
for all
\|\underline{i}\|\geq e
(some
e\in{\mathbb{Z}}
).
Fix any
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and suppose that
h(\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}}x)\neq 0
and
\|\underline{i}\|=M
.
By equations (
86
)–(
87
) we get that
h(\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}+\underline{k}}x)\neq 0
for some
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
such that
k_{j}\geq-\|\underline{k}\|
for all
j
.
In particular,
\|\underline{i}\|\leq\|\underline{i}\|+\|\underline{k}\|<e
, so

X^{\prime}_{M}\subseteq\bigcup_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k_{j}\geq-\|\underline{k}\|\;\forall j}}{{0\leq\|\underline{k}\|<e-M}}}\big(X_{M+\|\underline{k}\|}-\underline{k}\big),

a finite union of finite sets. The converse direction follows by reversing the roles of
Y_{j}
and
Z_{j}
.
∎

3.4
Some combinatorial lemmas and computations

We give several technical but important lemmas (some generalizing results in
[
BHH+
, §3.2]
) involving the combined action of
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}
(for some
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
) and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{0}\\
{0}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
in a representation
\pi
as at the end of §
3.1
.

We recall some notation and results from
[
BHH+
]
.
Let
H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}&0\\
0&\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\end{pmatrix}\leq\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.
As in
[
BP12
]
we write
(s_{0},s_{1},\dots,s_{f-1})\otimes\eta
for the Serre weight

\mathrm{Sym}^{s_{0}}\mathbb{F}^{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathrm{Sym}^{s_{1}}\mathbb{F}^{2})^{\rm Fr}\otimes\cdots\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}(\mathrm{Sym}^{s_{f-1}}\mathbb{F}^{2})^{{\rm Fr}^{f-1}}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}\eta\circ\det,

where the
s_{i}
are integers between
0
and
p-1
,
\eta
is a character
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{\times}
and
\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
acts on
(\mathrm{Sym}^{s_{i}}\mathbb{F}^{2})^{{\rm Fr}^{i}}
via
\sigma_{i}:\mathbb{F}_{q}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}
.
We fix
\overline{\rho}
as at the end of §
3.1
. We identify
W(\overline{\rho})
with the subsets of
\{0,\dots,f-1\}
as in
[
Bre11
, §2]
and let
J_{\sigma}
be the subset associated to
\sigma
. More precisely,
W(\overline{\rho})
is exactly the set of Serre weights of the form

(\lambda_{0}(r_{0}),\dots,\lambda_{f-1}(r_{f-1}))\otimes{\det}^{e(\lambda)(r_{0},\dots,r_{f-1})}

where
\lambda\in\mathcal{ID}(x_{0},\dots,x_{f-1})
(resp.
\lambda\in\mathcal{RD}(x_{0},\dots,x_{f-1})
) if
\overline{\rho}
is irreducible (resp.
\overline{\rho}
is reducible), see
[
BP12
, §11]
or
[
Bre11
, §2]
. If
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
corresponds to
\lambda
, then
we have
\lambda_{j}(x_{j})\in\{p-2-x_{j},p-3-x_{j}\}
if and only if
j\in J_{\sigma}
when
j>0
or
\overline{\rho}
is reducible,
\lambda_{0}(x_{0})\in\{p-2-x_{0},p-1-x_{0}\}
if and only if
0\in J_{\sigma}
when
\overline{\rho}
is irreducible.

Let
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
. Let
\delta(\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\delta_{\rm irr}(\sigma)
if
\overline{\rho}
is irreducible and
\delta(\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\delta_{\rm red}(\sigma)
if
\overline{\rho}
is reducible the Serre weights defined in
[
Bre11
, §5]
.
Then
\delta(\sigma)\in W(\overline{\rho})
and we have the following
explicit description of
J_{\delta(\sigma)}
(see
[
Bre11
, §5]
):

\begin{array}[]{rlll}j\in J_{\delta(\sigma)},j<f-1~(\mathrm{resp}.~f-1\in J_{\delta(\sigma)})&\!\!\!\Longleftrightarrow&j+1\in J_{\sigma}~(\mathrm{resp}.~0\notin J_{\sigma})&\!\!\mathrm{if}\ \delta=\delta_{\rm irr}\\
j\in J_{\delta(\sigma)}&\!\!\!\Longleftrightarrow&j+1\in J_{\sigma}&\!\!\mathrm{if}\ \delta=\delta_{\rm red}.\end{array}

We fix a nonzero vector
v_{\sigma}\in\sigma^{N_{0}}
, and let

\chi_{\sigma}:H\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{\times}
be the

H
-eigencharacter
of
v_{\sigma}
. Let
\chi_{\sigma}^{s}:H\rightarrow\mathbb{F}^{\times}
denote the conjugate of
\chi_{\sigma}
by
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{0}&{1}\\
{1}&{0}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
. As in
[
BP12
, §2]
we identify the irreducible constituents of
\Ind_{I}^{\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})}(\chi_{\sigma}^{s})
with the subsets of
\{0,\dots,f-1\}
(for example
\emptyset
corresponds to the socle
\sigma
of
\Ind_{I}^{\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})}(\chi_{\sigma}^{s})
). We know that
\delta(\sigma)
occurs in
\Ind_{I}^{\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})}(\chi_{\sigma}^{s})
and we denote by
J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\subseteq\{0,\dots,f-1\}
the associated subset. Precisely, using
[
BP12
, Lemma 2.7]
one checks that

J^{\rm max}(\sigma)=(J_{\sigma}\cup J_{\delta(\sigma)})\setminus(J_{\sigma}\cap J_{\delta(\sigma)}).

By
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.2.3.2]
, we have
|J^{\rm max}(\sigma)|=|J^{\rm max}(\delta(\sigma))|
. As a consequence, the quantity

m\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}|J^{\rm max}(\sigma)|\in\{0,\dots,f-1\}

depends only on the orbit of
\sigma
. By the proof of
[
BP12
, Lemma 19.5]
, the vector
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{0}&{1}\\
{p}&{0}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)v_{\sigma}
generates a
\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
-subrepresentation of
\pi
isomorphic to the unique quotient of
\Ind_{I}^{\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})}(\chi_{\sigma}^{s})
with irreducible socle parametrized by
J^{\rm max}(\sigma)
, which in particular yields an embedding of
\delta(\sigma)
in
\mathrm{soc}_{\GL_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{K})}(\pi)
.

Write

\sigma=(s_{0},\dots,s_{f-1})\otimes\eta,\ \ \delta(\sigma)=(s_{0}^{\prime},\dots,s_{f-1}^{\prime})\otimes\eta^{\prime}.

Define
\underline{c}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
by
c_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}s_{j}^{\prime}
if
j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)
, and
c_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}p-1
otherwise.

The following lemma explicitly determines
s_{j-1}^{\prime}
and
c_{j-1}
in terms of
s_{j}
. We remark that if
f=1
and
\overline{\rho}
is irreducible, some formulas need to be modified, e.g. Lemma
3.4.1
(i). But the main result (Theorem
3.1.3
) is known in this case, so it is harmless to exclude it.

Lemma 3.4.1
.

(i)

Assume that
\overline{\rho}
is irreducible and
f\geq 2
. Then

s_{0}

s_{f-1}^{\prime}

c_{f-1}

r_{0}

p-2-r_{f-1}

f-1\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

p-2-r_{f-1}

r_{0}-1

p-3-r_{f-1}

f-1\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

p-1

p-2-r_{0}

r_{f-1}

f-1\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

p-1

p-1-r_{0}

r_{f-1}+1

f-1\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

r_{f-1}+1

while if
1\leq j\leq f-1
we have

s_{j}

s_{j-1}^{\prime},~j=1

s_{j-1}^{\prime},~j>1

c_{j-1}

r_{j}

r_{0}-1

r_{j-1}

j-1\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

p-1

r_{j}+1

r_{0}

r_{j-1}+1

j-1\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

s_{j-1}^{\prime}

p-2-r_{j}

p-1-r_{0}

p-2-r_{j-1}

j-1\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

s_{j-1}^{\prime}

p-3-r_{j}

p-2-r_{0}

p-3-r_{j-1}

j-1\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

p-1

(ii)

Assume that
\overline{\rho}
is
(
split
)
reducible. Then for any
0\leq j\leq f-1
we have

s_{j}

s_{j-1}^{\prime}

c_{j-1}

r_{j}

r_{j-1}

j-1\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

p-1

r_{j}+1

r_{j-1}+1

j-1\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

r_{j-1}+1

p-2-r_{j}

p-2-r_{j-1}

j-1\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

p-2-r_{j-1}

p-3-r_{j}

p-3-r_{j-1}

j-1\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)

p-1

Proof.

This is an easy exercise using the relation between
J_{\sigma}
and
J_{\delta(\sigma)}
.
Note also that
j\not\in J_{\sigma}
if and only if
s_{j+1}\in\{r_{j+1},p-2-r_{j+1}\}
.
∎

Remark 3.4.2
.

Strictly speaking, we should state Lemma
3.4.1
in terms of
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
, which are the elements in
\mathcal{ID}(x_{0},\dots,x_{f-1})
or
\mathcal{RD}(x_{0},\dots,x_{f-1})
(depending on whether
\overline{\rho}
is irreducible or reducible) corresponding to
\sigma,\delta(\sigma)
respectively.
Lemma
3.4.1
determines
\lambda_{j-1}^{\prime}(x_{j-1})
in terms of
\lambda_{j}(x_{j})
, not
s^{\prime}_{j-1}
in terms of
s_{j}
(because ambiguities arise when
r_{0}=\frac{p-1}{2}
in the first table, and when
r_{j}=\frac{p-3}{2}
in the second and third tables.) The same comment applies to Lemma
3.4.7
.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

The vector
\underline{Y}^{\underline{c}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{\sigma})

spans
\delta(\sigma)^{N_{0}}
as an
\mathbb{F}
-vector space.
Hence there is a unique scalar
\mu_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
such that

v_{\delta(\sigma)}=\mu_{\sigma}\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{c}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{\sigma})

(88)

Proof.

This is
[
BHH+
, Prop. 3.2.3.1(i)]
.
∎

By
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.2.2.6(ii)]
, if
\underline{0}\leq\underline{i}\leq\underline{s}
, there is a unique
H
-eigenvector
\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{\sigma}\in\sigma
that is sent by
\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}}
to
v_{\sigma}
. The following result is a generalization of
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.2.3.5]
.

Lemma 3.4.4
.

Assume
m>0
. Let
\underline{k},\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
such that
\|\underline{i}\|\leq f-1
and
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{\sigma})\neq 0
.

(i)

We have

\|\underline{k}\|\leq p\|\underline{i}\|+\|\underline{c}\|.

(ii)

If
\|\underline{k}\|\geq p\|\underline{i}\|-(f-1)+\|\underline{c}\|
, then

\mu_{\sigma}\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{\sigma})=\underline{Y}^{-\underline{\ell}}v_{\delta(\sigma)}\in\delta(\sigma)

for some
\underline{\ell}\geq\underline{0}
with
\|\underline{\ell}\|\leq f-1
.
More precisely,
\ell_{j}=i_{j+1}p+c_{j}-k_{j}
for all
j
.

Proof.

Before starting the proof, we first remark that Lemma 3.2.3.3 and Lemma 3.2.3.4 of
[
BHH+
]
remain true if we replace the assumption
\|\underline{i}\|\leq m-1
by
\|\underline{i}\|\leq f-1
in the statements. Indeed, for Lemma 3.2.3.3, this new assumption
\|\underline{i}\|\leq f-1
implies
i_{j}\leq f-1
, and so

2i_{j}+1\leq 2f-1\leq s_{j}

for all
j
(
s_{j}
is denoted by
t_{j}
in
loc. cit.
) by the genericity assumption. Hence,
[
BHH+23
, Prop. 6.2.2]
still applies and the rest of the proof of Lemma 3.2.3.3 works without change. The proof of Lemma 3.2.3.4 of
[
BHH+
]
also works through, because one checks that besides the citation to Lemma 3.2.3.3 the condition
\|\underline{i}\|\leq m-1
is only used to deduce
\|\underline{i}\|\leq f-1
.

Now we prove the lemma, following the proof of
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.2.3.5]
. We first prove by induction on
\|\underline{i}\|\leq f-1
the following fact: if

\|\underline{k}\|\geq p\|\underline{i}\|-(f-1)+\|\underline{c}\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}B

and
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{\sigma})\neq 0
, then

\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{\sigma})=\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{\sigma})
for some
\underline{k}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
such that
k_{j}^{\prime}=k_{j}-i_{j+1}p
for all
j
. This is trivial if
\underline{i}=\underline{0}
, so we can assume
\underline{i}\neq\underline{0}
. Moreover, as in
loc. cit.
, by induction we are reduced to the case
k_{j}<p
for all
j
. We make this assumption and derive below a contradiction (so this case cannot happen).

Define a set
J
as in
loc. cit.
, i.e.

J\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\{j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma),i_{j+1}=0\}.

(89)

As in
loc. cit.
we have

\|\underline{k}\|\leq(p-1)(f-|J|)+\sum_{j\in J}(s_{j}^{\prime}+2i_{j})+|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}A

and to get a contradiction it is enough to show
A<B
, which is equivalent to

mp+|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|<(p-2)\|\underline{i}\|+(p-1)|J|+C+D,

(90)

where

C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}m-(f-1),\ \ D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}2\sum_{j\notin J}i_{j}+\sum_{j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus J}s_{j}^{\prime}.

We have the following two cases.

•

If
|J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus J|>0
, then as in
loc. cit.

m\leq\|\underline{i}\|+|J|
, hence (
90
) is implied by

mp+|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|<(p-2)\|\underline{i}\|+(p-2)(m-\|\underline{i}\|)+|J|+C+D,

or equivalently

m+(f-1)+|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|<|J|+D.

This is slightly stronger than (140) of
[
BHH+
]
, but one checks that the argument in
loc. cit.
still allows to conclude.

•

If
J^{\rm max}(\sigma)=J
, then as in
loc. cit.
we have
|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|\leq f-m
and
|J|=m
, and (
90
) is implied by

mp+(f-m)<(p-2)\|\underline{i}\|+(p-1)m+C+D

or equivalently

2f-1<(p-2)\|\underline{i}\|+m+D.

As
\|\underline{i}\|>0
and
D\geq 0
, the last inequality holds
by our genericity condition (i.e.
p>4f
).

This proves the desired fact. The rest of the proof is the same as the proof of
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.2.3.5]
and we omit the details.
(Several times
f-m=(f-1)-(m-1)
has to be added or subtracted from expressions in the last three paragraphs of the proof in
loc. cit.
to account for the weaker lower bound in Lemma
3.4.4
(ii).)
∎

Remark 3.4.5
.

Taking
\underline{i}=\underline{0}
in Lemma
3.4.4
, we get the following. If
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{\sigma})\neq 0
for some
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
and if

\|\underline{k}\|\geq\|\underline{c}\|-(f-1),

then

\mu_{\sigma}\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{\sigma})=\underline{Y}^{-\underline{\ell}}v_{\delta(\sigma)}\in\delta(\sigma)
for some
\|\underline{\ell}\|\leq f-1
.
More precisely,
\underline{\ell}=\underline{c}-\underline{k}
.

We will need the following analogue of Lemma
3.4.4
.

Lemma 3.4.6
.

Assume
m>0
. Let
\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
such that
\|\underline{i}\|\leq f-1
. Let
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
and assume that there exists
0\leq j_{0}\leq f-1
such that

(a)

k_{j_{0}}\leq p(i_{j_{0}+1}-1)
(hence
i_{j_{0}+1}\geq 1
);

(b)

\|\underline{k}\|>p\|\underline{i}\|+\|\underline{c}\|-c_{j_{0}}
.

Then
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{\sigma})=0
.

Proof.

Assume for a contradiction that
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{\sigma})\neq 0
.
As in the proof of
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.2.3.5]
, by induction we are reduced to the case
k_{j}<p
for all
j
; we make this assumption from now on.
Note
\|\underline{c}\|=\sum_{j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)}s_{j}^{\prime}+\sum_{j\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)}(p-1)
.

Let
J
be the set defined by (
89
).
Then by (a) we have
j_{0}\notin J
. As explained in the proof of Lemma
3.4.4
,
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.2.3.4]
still applies, and we get (see the fourth paragraph of the proof of Lemma 3.2.3.5 of
loc. cit.
)

\sum_{j\neq j_{0}}k_{j}\leq(f-1-|J|)(p-1)+\sum_{j\in J}(s_{j}^{\prime}+2i_{j})+|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}A.

On the other hand, letting
\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}1
if
i_{j_{0}+1}>1
and
\gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}0
if
i_{j_{0}+1}=1
we see that
k_{j_{0}}\leq(p-1)\gamma
(using (a) when
\gamma=0
),
which together with condition (b) implies

\sum_{j\neq j_{0}}k_{j}>p\|\underline{i}\|-(p-1)\gamma+\sum_{j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)}s_{j}^{\prime}+\sum_{j\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)}(p-1)-c_{j_{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}B.

To get a contradiction it is enough to show
A\leq B
.

A computation shows that
A\leq B
is equivalent to

\displaystyle mp+|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|

\displaystyle\leq(p-2)\|\underline{i}\|+(p-1)|J|+(p-1)(1-\gamma)+C+D

(91)

\displaystyle=(p-2)(\|\underline{i}\|+|J|+1-\gamma)+|J|+1-\gamma+C+D,

where

C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}m-c_{j_{0}},\ \ D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}2\sum_{j\notin J}i_{j}+\sum_{j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus J}s_{j}^{\prime}.

If
j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus J
, then
i_{j+1}>0
, so we obtain

\displaystyle|J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus J|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus(J\cup\{j_{0}\})}i_{j+1}+1=\bigg(\sum_{J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus(J\cup\{j_{0}\})}i_{j+1}\bigg)+i_{j_{0}+1}+(1-i_{j_{0}+1})

\displaystyle\leq\|\underline{i}\|+(1-i_{j_{0}+1}).

As
|J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus J|=m-|J|
and
i_{j_{0}+1}\geq\gamma+1
, this means

m\leq\|\underline{i}\|+|J|+(1-i_{j_{0}+1})\leq\|\underline{i}\|+|J|-\gamma.

Thus, to show (
91
) it is enough to show

mp+|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|\leq(p-2)(m+1)+|J|+1-\gamma+C+D

or equivalently

2m+|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|\leq|J|+(p-1-\gamma+C)+D.

If
|J^{\rm max}(\sigma)\setminus J|>0
, then it is true by
[
BHH+
, Eq. (140)]
(and using
p-2+m-c_{j_{0}}\geq 0
as
m\geq 1
). If
J^{\rm max}(\sigma)=J
, then again as in
loc. cit.
, we have
|J\setminus(J^{\rm max}(\sigma)+1)|\leq f-m
and
|J|=m
, and (
91
) is implied by

mp+f-m\leq(p-2)\|\underline{i}\|+(p-1)(m+1-\gamma)+(m-c_{j_{0}})+D,

equivalently,

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\leq(p-2)\|\underline{i}\|+(p-1)(1-\gamma)+(m-c_{j_{0}})+D

\displaystyle=(p-2)(\|i\|-\gamma)+(p-1-c_{j_{0}})+(m-\gamma)+D.

This is true by our genericity condition: indeed, as
\|\underline{i}\|\geq\gamma+1
,
m\geq 1
,
c_{j_{0}}\leq p-1
, and
D\geq 0
, the above inequality is implied by

f\leq p-2\leq p-1-\gamma
.
∎

Now, fix
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
and define
\sigma_{i}\in W(\overline{\rho})
inductively by
\sigma_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\sigma
and
\sigma_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\delta(\sigma_{i-1})
for
i\geq 1
. Let
d\geq 1
be the smallest integer such that
\sigma_{d}\cong\sigma_{0}
. For convenience, if
i\geq 0
we set
\sigma_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\sigma_{i^{\prime}}
, where
i^{\prime}\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
is the unique integer such that
i\equiv i^{\prime}\ (\mathrm{mod}\ d)
. Write

\sigma_{i}=(s_{0}^{(i)},\dots,s_{f-1}^{(i)})\otimes\eta_{i}.

To make the notation consistent, we also write
s_{j}=s_{j}^{(0)}
.

For convenience, we introduce the following notation. For
i\geq 1
, define
\underline{c_{i}^{\sigma}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
by

c_{i,j}^{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}s_{j}^{(i)}&\text{if $j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma_{i-1})$,}\\
p-1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(92)

(in particular
\underline{0}\leq\underline{c_{i}^{\sigma}}\leq\underline{p-1}
).
Define a shift function
\delta:{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}\to{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
by setting

\delta(\underline{i})_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}i_{j+1},\ \ \underline{i}=(i_{j})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}.

Note that
\delta
does not change
\|\cdot\|
and that

\underline{Y}^{p\delta(\underline{i})}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}}.

We inductively define
\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
for
n\geq 0
as follows:
\underline{a_{0}^{\sigma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\underline{0}
and for
n\geq 1
,

\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}p\delta(\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}})+\underline{c_{n}^{\sigma}}.

(93)

For example,
a_{1,j}^{\sigma}=c_{1,j}^{\sigma}=s_{j}^{(1)}
if
j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma)
and
a_{1,j}^{\sigma}=c_{1,j}^{\sigma}=p-1
if
j\notin J^{\rm max}(\sigma)
.

The following result determines
\underline{a_{d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}
in terms of the
s_{j}
(where
d^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}df
).
For
0\leq j\leq f-1
, recall that
h_{j}=r_{j}+1
and define

h^{[j]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}h_{j}+ph_{j+1}+\cdots+p^{f-1-j}h_{f-1}

(94)

(thus
h^{[0]}=h
).

Lemma 3.4.7
.

(i)

Assume that
\overline{\rho}
is irreducible and
f\geq 2
. Then

s_{0}

r_{0}

r_{0}-1

p-2-r_{0}

p-1-r_{0}

\frac{a_{d^{\prime},0}^{\sigma}}{1-p^{d^{\prime}}}

-1+\frac{h}{1+q}

-1

-1

-\frac{h}{1+q}

while if
1\leq j\leq f-1
we have

s_{j}

r_{j}

r_{j}+1

p-2-r_{j}

p-3-r_{j}

\frac{a_{d^{\prime},j}^{\sigma}}{1-p^{d^{\prime}}}

-1

h^{[j]}-\frac{hp^{f-j}}{1+q}

-1-h^{[j]}+\frac{hp^{f-j}}{1+q}

-1

(ii)

Assume that
\overline{\rho}
is
(
split
)
reducible. Then for any
0\leq j\leq f-1
:

s_{j}

r_{j}

r_{j}+1

p-2-r_{j}

p-3-r_{j}

\frac{a_{d^{\prime},j}^{\sigma}}{1-p^{d^{\prime}}}

-1

h^{[j]}+\frac{hp^{f-j}}{1-q}

-1-h^{[j]}-\frac{hp^{f-j}}{1-q}

-1

Proof.

(i) Note that we always have
2|d
(as
d\nmid f
but
d|(2f)
) and so
(2f)|d^{\prime}
. Thus it suffices to prove the formulas for
\frac{a_{2f,j}^{\sigma}}{1-p^{2f}}
; we choose to work with
2f
because
d|(2f)
by
[
Bre11
, Lem. 5.2]
. Using Lemma
3.4.1
, we can inductively determine
c_{n,j}^{\sigma}
for
1\leq n\leq 2f
, and then compute
a_{2f,j}^{\sigma}
using the formula
a_{2f,j}^{\sigma}=\sum_{k=0}^{2f-1}p^{k}c_{2f-k,j+k}^{\sigma}
, where
c^{\sigma}_{n,j}
is understood to be
c^{\sigma}_{n,j~(\mathrm{mod}\ f)}
if
j\geq f
.

We do this in the case
j=0
and
s_{0}=r_{0}
, and leave the other cases to the reader. In this case, we obtain using Lemma
3.4.1
that

c^{\sigma}_{1,f-1}=p-2-r_{f-1},~\dots,~c^{\sigma}_{f-1,1}=p-2-r_{1},~c^{\sigma}_{f,0}=p-1-r_{0},

c^{\sigma}_{f+1,f-1}=r_{f-1}+1,~\dots,~c^{\sigma}_{2f-1,1}=r_{1}+1,~c^{\sigma}_{2f,0}=r_{0},

and so

\begin{array}[]{rll}a_{2f,0}^{\sigma}&=&r_{0}+p(r_{1}+1)+\cdots+p^{f-1}(r_{f-1}+1)\\
&&+p^{f}(p-1-r_{0})+p^{f+1}(p-2-r_{1})+\cdots+p^{2f-1}(p-2-r_{f-1})\\
&=&(h-1)+p^{f}(p^{f}-h)\\
&=&(1-p^{2f})(-1+\frac{h}{1+p^{f}}),\end{array}

proving the result.

(ii) In this case it suffices to prove the formulas for
\frac{a_{f,j}^{\sigma}}{1-p^{f}}
. The computation is similar to (i) and is easier, and we leave it to the reader.
∎

For
i\geq 0
let

v_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}v_{\delta^{i}(\sigma)}\in\delta^{i}(\sigma)^{N_{0}}\setminus\{0\}

and

\mu_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\mu_{\delta^{i}(\sigma)}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
, as defined in Lemma
3.4.3
. Then by (
88
) we have

v_{i}=\mu_{i-1}\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{c_{i}^{\sigma}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{i-1})\quad\forall\ i\geq 1.

(95)

Let

\lambda_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}(-1)^{d(f-1)}\big(\prod_{0\leq i^{\prime}\leq d-1}\prod_{j\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma_{i^{\prime}})}(p-1-s_{j}^{(i^{\prime}+1)})!\big)^{-1}\nu_{\sigma},

(96)

where
\nu_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
is defined as before
[
BHH+
, Prop. 3.2.4.2]
, i.e. the eigenvalue of the operator
S^{d}
defined in
[
Bre11
, §4]
acting on
\sigma^{I_{1}}
. Note that
\nu_{\sigma}
depends only on the orbit of
\sigma
, and hence the same is true for
\lambda_{\sigma}
.

Lemma 3.4.8
.

We have

\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\mu_{i}=\lambda_{\sigma}^{-1}.

Proof.

This follows from
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.2.2.5]
and the definition of
\nu_{\sigma}
.
∎

Remark 3.4.9
.

When
\pi
moreover comes from cohomology, i.e. is as in (
63
) or (
64
), it is conjectured in
[
Bre11
, §6]
and proved in
[
DL21
, Thm. 5.36]
that

•

if
\overline{\rho}
is irreducible, then
\nu_{\sigma}=(-1)^{\frac{dh}{2f}(1+\sum_{j=0}^{f-1}r_{j})}(-\det(\overline{\rho})(p))^{\frac{d}{2}}
;

•

if
\overline{\rho}
is reducible, then
\nu_{\sigma}=(-1)^{\frac{dh}{f}\sum_{j=0}^{f-1}r_{j}}\lambda_{0}^{|\overline{J_{\sigma}}|\frac{d}{f}}\lambda_{1}^{|J_{\sigma}|\frac{d}{f}}
, where
J_{\sigma}\subseteq\{0,1,\dots,f-1\}
is the set corresponding to
\sigma
and
\overline{J_{\sigma}}
denotes its complement.

Here,
h
is the number attached to
\sigma
in
[
Bre11
, Lemma 6.2]
(it is not the integer
h
of §
3.1
). By the proof of
[
Bre11
, Lemma 6.2]
, we deduce

\lambda_{\sigma}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}(-1)^{d(f-1)}(-\det(\overline{\rho})(p))^{\frac{d}{2}}&\text{if $\overline{\rho}$ irreducible,}\\
(-1)^{d(f-1)}\lambda_{0}^{|\overline{J_{\sigma}}|\frac{d}{f}}\lambda_{1}^{|J_{\sigma}|\frac{d}{f}}&\text{if $\overline{\rho}$ reducible}.\end{array}\right.

(97)

The following result follows by induction from (
95
), as well as Lemma
3.4.8
.

Lemma 3.4.10
.

For all
n\geq 0
, we have
\big(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}\mu_{i}\big)\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})=v_{n}
.
In particular, for all
n\geq 0
,
\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd}^{\sigma}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd}(v_{\sigma})=\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}v_{\sigma}
.

Proposition 3.4.11
.

Let
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
and
n\geq 0
. If
\|\underline{k}\|\geq\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|-(f-1)
and
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})\neq 0
, then
\underline{k}=\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}-\underline{\ell}
for some
\underline{\ell}\geq\underline{0}
satisfying
\|\underline{\ell}\|\leq f-1
and

\big(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}\mu_{i}\big)\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})=\underline{Y}^{-\underline{\ell}}v_{n}\in\sigma_{n}.

Proof.

If
n=0
, we necessarily have
\underline{k}=\underline{a_{0}^{\sigma}}=\underline{\ell}=\underline{0}
and there is nothing to prove. Assume
n\geq 1
and that the statement holds for
n-1
.

Let
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
with
\|\underline{k}\|\geq\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|-(f-1)
. Write
\underline{k}=p\delta(\underline{k}^{\prime})+\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}
, with
\underline{k}^{\prime}\geq\underline{0}

and
\underline{0}\leq\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\leq\underline{p-1}
.
Recalling that
\|\delta(\cdot)\|=\|\cdot\|
, the assumption implies the following inequalities

p\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|+(p-1)f\geq\|\underline{k}\|>\|\underline{a}_{n}^{\sigma}\|-f\geq p\|\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}\|-f,

from which we deduce
\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|>\|\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}\|-f
, equivalently

\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|\geq\|\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}\|-(f-1).

We clearly have

\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})=\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\big(\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n-1}(v_{0})\big),

(98)

so in particular
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n-1}(v_{0})\neq 0
. As
\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|\geq\|\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}\|-(f-1)
, by the inductive hypothesis there exists
\underline{\ell}^{\prime}\geq\underline{0}
with
\|\underline{\ell}^{\prime}\|\leq f-1
such that

\underline{k}^{\prime}=\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}-\underline{\ell}^{\prime}\text{\ \ and\ \ }\big(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\mu_{i}\big)\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n-1}(v_{0})=\underline{Y}^{-\underline{\ell}^{\prime}}v_{n-1}\in\sigma_{n-1}.

(99)

We first assume
m>0
and claim that
\underline{\ell}^{\prime}=\underline{0}
. Indeed, the relation
\|\underline{k}\|\geq\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|-(f-1)
together with (
99
) gives

\|\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\|\geq p\|\underline{\ell}^{\prime}\|-(f-1)+\|\underline{c_{n}^{\sigma}}\|.

Lemma
3.4.4
(ii) applied with
\sigma=\sigma_{n-1}
(and genericity) shows that
k_{j}^{\prime\prime}\geq\ell^{\prime}_{j+1}p
for all
j
. However, by definition
0\leq k_{j}^{\prime\prime}\leq p-1
, so we must have
\ell^{\prime}_{j+1}=0
for all
j
. This proves the claim.

By the claim and by equations (
98
)–(
99
) we have
\underline{k}^{\prime}=\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}
and

\big(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\mu_{i}\big)\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n-1}(v_{0})=v_{n-1},\text{\ \ so\ \ }\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{n-1})\neq 0.

By the previous paragraph we have moreover that
\|\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\|\geq\|\underline{c_{n}^{\sigma}}\|-(f-1)
.
Remark
3.4.5
applied with
\sigma=\sigma_{n-1}
gives
\mu_{n-1}\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{n-1})=\underline{Y}^{-\underline{\ell}}v_{n}\in\sigma_{n}

for some
\underline{\ell}\geq\underline{0}
satisfying
\|\underline{\ell}\|\leq f-1
and
\underline{\ell}=\underline{c_{n}^{\sigma}}-\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}
. As
\underline{k}^{\prime}=\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}
we deduce
\underline{\ell}=\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}-\underline{k}
and the result follows.

Now we assume
m=0
, equivalently
\sigma\cong\delta(\sigma)
. It is easy to see that this case only happens when
\overline{\rho}
is (split) reducible and either
J_{\sigma}=\emptyset
or
J_{\sigma}=\{0,\dots,f-1\}
. In this case we have
\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}=\underline{p^{n}-1}
for any
n\geq 0
, and Lemma
3.4.3
implies that
\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})\neq 0
. Using (
98
) and the fact
Y_{j}v_{0}=0
for all
j
, an induction shows that if
k_{j}\geq p^{n}
for some
0\leq j\leq f-1
, then

\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})=\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}-p^{n}\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}\big(\underline{Y}^{\delta^{-n}(\underline{k}^{\prime})}v_{0}\big)=0,

where
k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
is defined as:
k^{\prime}_{j}=1
and
k^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}=0
for
j^{\prime}\neq j
. We deduce that
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})\neq 0
if and only if
\underline{k}\leq\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}
, which implies the first assertion. The second assertion can be proved as above, noting that Remark
3.4.5
remains true when
m=0
.
∎

Corollary 3.4.12
.

Let
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
and
n\geq 0
.

(i)

If
\|\underline{k}\|>\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|
, then
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})=0
.

(ii)

If
\|\underline{k}\|=\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|
and if
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})\neq 0
, then

\underline{k}=\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}
.

Proof.

It is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.4.11
.
∎

3.5
The degree function on an admissible smooth representation of
\GL_{2}(K)

We define and study a “degree function” on representations
\pi
as at the end of §
3.1
.

Let
\overline{\rho}
and
\pi
be as in
loc. cit.
We define
\gr(\pi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{n+1}]/\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{n}]
. For
v\in\pi
, we define

\deg(v)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\min\{n\geq-1:\ v\in\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{n+1}]\}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq-1}.

We let
\gr(v)
be the image of
v
in
\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{\deg(v)+1}]/\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{\deg(v)}]
if
v\neq 0
and
\gr(v)=0
if
v=0
.

Fix
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
and let
v_{\sigma}\in\sigma^{N_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
. Define
\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
as in §
3.4
.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

For all
n\geq 0
we have

\deg\big(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{\sigma})\big)=\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|.

Proof.

Put
u_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{\sigma})
for simplicity. First, by the proof of
[
BHH+23
, Cor. 5.3.5]
, we know that as a
\gr(\mathbb{F}\llbracket I_{1}/Z_{1}\rrbracket)
-module
\gr(\pi)
is annihilated by the ideal
J
defined by
J\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}(y_{j}z_{j},z_{j}y_{j};0\leq j\leq f-1)
, so that
\gr(\pi)
becomes a graded module over
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\gr(\mathbb{F}\llbracket I_{1}/Z_{1}\rrbracket)/J
which is commutative, isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}[y_{j},z_{j}]/(y_{j}z_{j};0\leq j\leq f-1)
, with
y_{j}
,
z_{j}
of degree
-1
. On the other hand, as
v_{\sigma}\in\sigma^{N_{0}}=\sigma^{I_{1}}
, it is direct to check that
u_{n}
is fixed by
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{0}\\
{p\mathcal{O}_{K}}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
for all
n\geq 0
, hence

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}\lambda^{-p^{j}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{0}\\
{p[\lambda]}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)u_{n}=\big(\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}\lambda^{-p^{j}}\big)u_{n}=0.

Namely,

u_{n}
is annihilated by
\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}\lambda^{-p^{j}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{0}\\
{p[\lambda]}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\in\mathbb{F}\llbracket I_{1}/Z_{1}\rrbracket
(a lifting of
z_{j}
),
hence
z_{j}\gr(u_{n})=0
and consequently we observe that any element in
\langle R\cdot\gr(u_{n})\rangle
is annihilated by
z_{j}
.

Next we note the following fact: if
v\in\pi
with
\deg(v)>0
and if
\gr(v)
is annihilated by all
z_{j}
, then there exists some
i\in\{0,\dots,f-1\}
such that
y_{i}\gr(v)\neq 0
. (If not, then
R_{-1}
, the degree
-1
part of
R
, annihilates
\gr(v)
. Suppose
v\in\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{n+1}]\setminus\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{n}]
for some
n\geq 1
. Since
R_{-1}=\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}/\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{2}
, we deduce
\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}v\subseteq\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{n-1}]
, i.e.
v\in\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{n}]
, contradiction.)
As a consequence,
Y_{i}v\neq 0
and

\deg(Y_{i}v)=\deg(v)-1;

moreover we have
\gr(Y_{i}v)=y_{i}\gr(v)\in\langle R\cdot\gr(v)\rangle
. Applying this fact to
u_{n}
(and to
Y_{i}u_{n}
, etc.) and using the observation of the last paragraph, we find that there exists
\underline{a_{n}^{\prime\sigma}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
such that
\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{n}^{\prime\sigma}}}u_{n}
is of degree 0, i.e.
\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{n}^{\prime\sigma}}}u_{n}\in\pi^{I_{1}}\setminus\{0\}
and

\deg(u_{n})=\|\underline{a_{n}^{\prime\sigma}}\|.

On the one hand, we have
\|\underline{a_{n}^{\prime\sigma}}\|\leq\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|
by Corollary
3.4.12
(i) (as
\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{n}^{\prime\sigma}}}u_{n}\neq 0
). On the other hand, we have
\deg(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{\sigma}))\geq\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|
by Lemma
3.4.10
, so the result follows.
∎

If
V
is any admissible smooth representation of
\GL_{2}(K)
over
\mathbb{F}
, we define
\deg(v)
for
v\in V
as above. On the other hand, by restricting
V
to
N_{0}
, we can also define

\deg^{\prime}(v)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\min\{n\geq-1:v\in V[\mathfrak{m}_{N_{0}}^{n+1}]\}.

This is well-defined as
V
is smooth. It is clear that
\deg(v)\geq\deg^{\prime}(v)
.

We note the following consequence of the proof of Proposition
3.5.1
(it will not be used in this paper).

Corollary 3.5.2
.

Let
V
be in the category
\mathcal{C}
of §
3.1
and assume that
\gr(V)
is annihilated by the ideal
J
defined in the proof of Proposition
3.5.1
. If
v\in V
is an element fixed by
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{1}&{0}\\
{p\mathcal{O}_{K}}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
, then
there exists
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
with
|\!|\underline{k}|\!|=\deg(v)
such that
0\neq\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}v\in V^{I_{1}}
. Moreover, we have

\deg(v)=\deg^{\prime}(v)
.

3.6
A crucial finiteness result

We prove an important finiteness result (Proposition
3.6.1
) which will be crucially used in §
3.7
to construct elements of
\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)
.

Fix
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
and define
\sigma_{i}\in W(\overline{\rho})
,
v_{i}\in\sigma_{i}
and
d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
as in §
3.4
(before Lemma
3.4.8
).
We have elements
\underline{c_{n}^{\sigma}},\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
defined for
n\geq 1
(resp.
n\geq 0
) in (
92
) (resp. (
93
)).
By induction we have

\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}p^{i}\delta^{i}(\underline{c_{n-i}^{\sigma}})

and as
\underline{c_{n}^{\sigma}}
is periodic with period
d
, we deduce

\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}=p^{d^{\prime}}\underline{a_{(n-1)d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}+\underline{a_{d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}},

(100)

where we recall that
d^{\prime}=df
(so
\delta^{d^{\prime}}
is the identity).

We consider the following elements for
\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
:

x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd}^{\sigma}}-\underline{i}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd}(v_{\sigma}),

where
\lambda_{\sigma}
is defined in (
96
) and
n\geq 0
is chosen large enough so that
\underline{a_{nd}^{\sigma}}-\underline{i}\geq\underline{0}
. By Lemma
3.4.10
the definition is independent of the choice of
n
.

The following finiteness result is the main result of this section.

Proposition 3.6.1
.

For any
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
the set
\{\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}:x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}\neq 0,\|\underline{i}\|=M\}
is finite.

For Lemmas
3.6.2
and
3.6.3
below, we assume
m=|J^{\rm max}(\sigma)|>0
.

Lemma 3.6.2
.

Let
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
and
n\geq 1
. Assume that for some
0\leq j_{0}\leq f-1
,

(a)

k_{j_{0}}\leq a_{n,j_{0}}^{\sigma}-p-c_{n,j_{0}}^{\sigma}
,

(b)

\|\underline{k}\|>\|\underline{a_{n}^{\sigma}}\|-c_{n,j_{0}}^{\sigma}
.

Then
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})=0
.

Proof.

Write
\underline{k}=p\delta(\underline{k}^{\prime})+\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}
with
\underline{k}^{\prime},\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\geq\underline{0}
and
\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\leq\underline{p-1}
.
Condition (b) implies

p\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|+(p-1)f\geq\|\underline{k}\|>p\|\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}\|+\sum_{j\neq j_{0}}c_{n,j}^{\sigma}

and consequently

p\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|+pf>p\|\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}\|.

Thus, we have
\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|>\|\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}\|-f
.

Assume
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n}(v_{0})\neq 0
for a contradiction. Then by the proof of Proposition
3.4.11
we also have
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n-1}(v_{0})\neq 0
. Moreover, by Proposition
3.4.11
, there exists
\underline{i}\geq\underline{0}
with
\|\underline{i}\|\leq f-1
such that
\underline{k}^{\prime}=\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}-\underline{i}
and

\big(\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-2}\mu_{i}\big)\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{{k}}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n-1}(v_{0})=\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{n-1}\in\sigma_{n-1}.

Thus, condition (a) translates to

p(a_{n-1,j_{0}+1}^{\sigma}-i_{j_{0}+1})+k_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime}\leq a_{n,j_{0}}^{\sigma}-p-c_{n,j_{0}}^{\sigma}

from which we deduce

k_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime}\leq p(i_{j_{0}+1}-1)
using (
93
),
and we get a contradiction by Lemma
3.4.6
applied to
\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}
. Indeed,
\underline{Y}^{\underline{{k}}^{\prime\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}v_{n-1})\neq 0
and the equality
\underline{k}^{\prime}=\underline{a_{n-1}^{\sigma}}-\underline{i}
together with condition (b) imply

\|\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\|>p\|\underline{i}\|+\|c_{n}^{\sigma}\|-c_{n,j_{0}}^{\sigma}

which verifies the corresponding condition (b) of Lemma
3.4.6
(with
\sigma=\sigma_{n-1}
).
∎

Recall that
d^{\prime}=df
, that
\delta^{d^{\prime}}
is the identity, and that
\underline{c_{n}^{\sigma}}
is periodic with period
d
.

Lemma 3.6.3
.

Let
\underline{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
and
n^{\prime}>n\geq 0
. Assume that for some
0\leq j_{0}\leq f-1
,

(a)

k_{j_{0}}\leq a_{n^{\prime}d^{\prime},j_{0}}^{\sigma}-a_{nd^{\prime},j_{0}}^{\sigma}-p^{nd^{\prime}}(p+c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma})
and

(b)

\|\underline{k}\|>\|\underline{a_{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|-\|\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|-p^{nd^{\prime}}c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma}+f(p^{nd^{\prime}}-1)
.

Then
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}(v_{0})=0
.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
3.6.2
with
n\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime}
, we see that if
\underline{k}^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}^{f}
such that
k^{\prime}_{j_{0}}\leq a_{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime},j_{0}}^{\sigma}-p-c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma}
(recall that
c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma}
is periodic) and if
\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|>\|\underline{a_{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|-c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma}
, then
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime}}(v_{0})=0
.

Write
\underline{k}=p^{nd^{\prime}}\underline{k}^{\prime}+\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}
with
\underline{k}^{\prime},\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\geq\underline{0}
and
\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\leq\underline{p^{nd^{\prime}}-1}
. Note that
\underline{a_{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}-\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}=p^{nd^{\prime}}\underline{a_{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}
by (
100
). Firstly, by condition (a) we have

p^{nd^{\prime}}k^{\prime}_{j_{0}}\leq k_{j_{0}}\leq p^{nd^{\prime}}a_{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime},j_{0}}^{\sigma}-p^{nd^{\prime}}(p+c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma})

and so
k_{j_{0}}^{\prime}\leq a_{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime},j_{0}}^{\sigma}-p-c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma}
. Secondly, as
f(p^{nd^{\prime}}-1)-\|\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}\|\geq 0
, condition (b) implies that

p^{nd^{\prime}}\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|>p^{nd^{\prime}}\|\underline{a_{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|-p^{nd^{\prime}}c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma}

so that

\|\underline{k}^{\prime}\|>\|\underline{a_{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|-c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma}.

We then conclude that
\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime}}(v_{0})=0
as explained above, hence

\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}(v_{0})=\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd^{\prime}}\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{(n^{\prime}-n)d^{\prime}}(v_{0})=0.\qed

Proof of Proposition
3.6.1
.

If
m=0
, then the end of the proof of Proposition
3.4.11
implies
x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}=0
if
i_{j}<0
for some
0\leq j\leq f-1
, from which the result easily follows.

Assume
m>0
from now on, so that Lemma
3.6.3
applies. Fix any
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. We will show that the set
\{\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}:x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}\neq 0,\|\underline{i}\|=M\}
is finite.
Choose
n
large enough such that for all
0\leq j\leq f-1
:

\|\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|+p^{nd^{\prime}}c_{d,j}^{\sigma}-f(p^{nd^{\prime}}-1)>M;

(101)

this is always possible because the left-hand side tends to infinity when

n\rightarrow\infty
(recall that
c_{d,j}^{\sigma}>f
, by genericity).

Now pick any
\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
such that
\|\underline{i}\|=M
. Choose
n^{\prime}>n
large enough such that
\underline{a_{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\geq\underline{i}
,
hence
x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}-\underline{i}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}(v_{0})
. By (
101
) and as
\|\underline{i}\|=M
, we get for all
0\leq j\leq f-1
:

\|\underline{a_{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|-\|\underline{i}\|>\|\underline{a_{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|-(\|\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|+p^{nd^{\prime}}c_{d,j}^{\sigma}-f(p^{nd^{\prime}}-1)).

There are two cases:

•

If
i_{j_{0}}\geq a_{nd^{\prime},j_{0}}^{\sigma}+p^{nd^{\prime}}(p+c_{d,j_{0}}^{\sigma})
for some
j_{0}
, then
x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}=0
by Lemma
3.6.3
(applied to
\underline{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\underline{a_{n^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}-\underline{i}
).

•

Otherwise, we must have
i_{j}<a_{nd^{\prime},j}^{\sigma}+p^{nd^{\prime}}(p+c_{d,j}^{\sigma})
for all
j
, and such a set (together with the restriction
\|\underline{i}\|=M
) is automatically finite. Note that the quantities
a_{nd^{\prime},j}^{\sigma}+p^{nd^{\prime}}(p+c_{d,j}^{\sigma})
depend only on our fixed
M
, as
n
does. ∎

3.7
An explicit basis of
\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)

We exhibit an
A
-basis of
\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)
and explicitly describe its image in the vector space
\Hom_{\mathbb{F}}^{\cont}(D_{A}(\pi),\mathbb{F})
via the embedding (
84
).

Recall
\pi
and
\overline{\rho}
are as in Theorem
3.1.3
with
\overline{\rho}
as at the end of §
3.1
, in particular
\pi^{I_{1}}
is multiplicity-free for the action of
I
. For any
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
and our fixed choice of
v_{\sigma}\in\sigma^{N_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
we define:

x_{\sigma,k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd}^{\sigma}}-\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd}v_{\sigma}

(102)

for
k\geq 0
and any
n\gg_{k}0
.
This is well-defined by Lemma
3.4.10
.

Recall from Proposition
3.5.1
that

\text{$\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd}v_{\sigma}\in\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{\|\underline{a_{nd}^{\sigma}}\|+1}]$,\ \ \ so $x_{\sigma,k}\in\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{kf+1}]$},

hence by Proposition
3.2.3
the sequence
(x_{\sigma,k})_{k\geq 0}
defines an element
x_{\sigma}
of
\Hom_{\mathbb{F}}^{\cont}(D_{A}(\pi),\mathbb{F})
of degree 0.

Theorem 3.7.1
.

The elements
\{x_{\sigma}:\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})\}
are contained in the image of the injection

\mu_{*}:\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)\hookrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbb{F}}^{\cont}(D_{A}(\pi),\mathbb{F})

and form an
A
-basis of
\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)
.

We first need a lemma. Note that
\pi^{I_{1}}
is multiplicity-free for the action of
I
, so there
exist unique
I
-eigenvectors
v_{\sigma}^{*}\in(\pi^{I_{1}})^{\vee}=\gr_{0}(\pi^{\vee})
such that
\langle v_{\sigma},v_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{*}\rangle=\delta_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}

(for
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in W(\overline{\rho})
). We already know that
D_{A}(\pi)
is free by Remark
2.6.2
. The following result only applies to our current
\pi
but is more precise.

Lemma 3.7.2
.

Suppose that
\pi
is as above. Then
\gr(D_{A}(\pi))
is a free
\gr(A)
-module with basis
(v_{\sigma}^{*})_{\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})}
.
In particular,
D_{A}(\pi)
is a filtered free
A
-module of rank
2^{f}
.

Proof.

Recall from
[
BHH+
, §3.1]
that
\gr(D_{A}(\pi))
is obtained from
\gr(\pi^{\vee})
by localizing at
\prod_{j}y_{j}
.
By localizing the surjection in
[
BHH+
, Thm. 3.3.2.1]
at
\prod_{j}y_{j}
and using
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.3.1.3(i)]
we obtain a surjection

\bigoplus_{\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})}\gr(A)\twoheadrightarrow\gr(D_{A}(\pi))
of
\gr(A)
-modules, sending the standard basis element indexed by
\sigma
on the left to

v_{\sigma}^{*}
. But
\rank_{\gr(A)}(\gr(D_{A}(\pi)))=\rank_{A}(D_{A}(\pi))=2^{f}
by
[
BHH+
, Lemma 3.1.4.1]
and
[
BHH+
, Cor. 3.3.2.4]
, hence the surjection
\bigoplus_{\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})}\gr(A)\twoheadrightarrow\gr(D_{A}(\pi))
is an isomorphism. By
[
LvO96
, Thm. I.4.2.4(5)]

we can lift it to an isomorphism
\bigoplus_{\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})}A\xrightarrow{\,\sim\,}D_{A}(\pi)
of filtered
A
-modules.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.7.1
.

Fix any
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
and consider the continuous
\mathbb{F}
-linear map
h_{\sigma}(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}x_{\sigma}):D_{A}(\pi)\to\mathbb{F}
of degree 0 corresponding to the sequence
(x_{\sigma,k})_{k\geq 0}
.
We endow
D_{A}(\pi)
with its natural good filtration (coming from the
\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}
-adic filtration on
\pi^{\vee}
, cf.
[
BHH+
, §3.1.2]
).
Let again
S
denote the multiplicative subset of
\mathbb{F}\llbracket N_{0}\rrbracket
generated by
Y_{0}\cdots Y_{f-1}
.
To descend
h_{\sigma}
to
\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)
we now check the second criterion in Lemma
3.3.6
. Thus fix any
x\in D_{A}(\pi)
and
M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By continuity there exists
e\in{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
h_{\sigma}(F_{e}D_{A}(\pi))=0
. As
(\pi^{\vee})_{S}
is dense in
D_{A}(\pi)
we can find
\underline{\ell}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
and
x^{*}\in\pi^{\vee}

such that
x-\underline{Y}^{\underline{\ell}}x^{*}\in F_{e+\|\underline{i}\|}D_{A}(\pi)
. Then
h_{\sigma}((x-\underline{Y}^{\underline{\ell}}x^{*})\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}})=0
for all
\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
such
that
\|\underline{i}\|=M
, so we may assume that
x=\underline{Y}^{\underline{\ell}}x^{*}\in(\pi^{\vee})_{S}
.

As in §
3.6
we define
x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd}^{\sigma}}-\underline{i}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd}(v_{\sigma})
for
\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
,
where
n\gg_{\underline{i}}0
. (In particular,
x_{\sigma,(k,\dots,k)}=x_{\sigma,k}
for
k\geq 0
and
\underline{Y}^{\underline{j}}x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}=x_{\sigma,\underline{i}-\underline{j}}
for any
\underline{j}\geq\underline{0}
.)
Explicitly,

h_{\sigma}\circ\underline{Y}^{-\underline{k}}=\langle x_{\sigma,k},-\rangle\quad\text{on $\pi^{\vee}$}

for all
k\geq 0
, from which it follows from the properties of
(x_{\sigma,\underline{i}})_{\underline{i}}
that

h_{\sigma}\circ\underline{Y}^{-\underline{i}}=\langle x_{\sigma,\underline{i}},-\rangle\quad\text{on $\pi^{\vee}$}

(103)

for all
\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
.
This implies that

h_{\sigma}(\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}}x)=h_{\sigma}(\underline{Y}^{\underline{i}+\underline{\ell}}x^{*})=x^{*}(x_{\sigma,-(\underline{i}+\underline{\ell})})

which can be nonzero for only finitely many
\underline{i}
by Proposition
3.6.1
. Thus
h_{\sigma}
indeed descends to an element

H_{\sigma}
of
\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)
.

For the final claim, first note that

\gr\big(\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)\big)\cong\Hom_{\gr(A)}\big(\gr(D_{A}(\pi)),\gr(A)\big)

by
[
LvO96
, Lemma I.6.9]
and Lemma
3.7.2
.
By
[
LvO96
, Cor. I.4.2.5(2)]
it then suffices to show that the
\gr(H_{\sigma})
(
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
) form a basis of

\Hom_{\gr(A)}(\gr(D_{A}(\pi)),\gr(A))
. By Lemma
3.7.2
, the
\gr(A)
-module
\gr(D_{A}(\pi))
has basis

v_{\sigma}^{*}
(
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
), so it will be enough to establish

\langle\gr(H_{\sigma}),v_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{*}\rangle=\delta_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}\underline{y}^{-\underline{1}}
for all
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in W(\overline{\rho})
.

By the explicit formula from the proof of Lemma
3.3.6
we know that

H_{\sigma}(x)=(\prod_{j}(1+T_{j}))\sum_{\underline{i}}h_{\sigma}(\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}}x)\underline{Z}^{-\underline{i}-\underline{1}}\quad\forall\ x\in D_{A}(\pi).

Consider the equality
\mu\circ H_{\sigma}=h_{\sigma}
.
Note that
H_{\sigma}
is a filtered map of degree
f
, since

h_{\sigma}
is of degree 0,
\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}}\in F_{-\|\underline{i}\|}A
, and
\prod_{j}(1+T_{j})\in F_{0}A
. Similarly,

\mu
is a filtered map of degree
-f
. Therefore

\gr(\mu)\circ\gr(H_{\sigma})=\gr(h_{\sigma}).

(104)

Recall that
\gr(A)=\mathbb{F}[y_{0}^{\pm 1},\dots,y_{f-1}^{\pm 1}]
. Let
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\underline{i}}:\gr(A)\to\mathbb{F}
be the map sending

\sum_{\underline{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}}\lambda_{\underline{j}}\underline{y}^{\underline{j}}
to
\lambda_{\underline{i}}
; it is
\mathbb{F}
-linear and of degree
\|\underline{i}\|
.
By definition,
\gr(\mu):\gr_{f}A\to\mathbb{F}
sends
\gr(\prod_{j}(1+T_{j})\sum_{\|\underline{i}\|\geq-f}\lambda_{\underline{i}}\underline{Z}^{\underline{i}})
to
\lambda_{-\underline{1}}
.
As
\gr(Y_{j})=\gr(Z_{j})
, it follows that

\gr(\mu)=\overline{\varepsilon}_{-\underline{1}}.

On the other hand, relation (
103
) implies that

\gr(h_{\sigma})\circ\underline{y}^{-\underline{i}}=\langle\gr(x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}),-\rangle\quad\text{on $\gr(\pi^{\vee})$}

(105)

for all
\underline{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{f}
. (They are graded maps of degree
\|\underline{i}\|
; we filter
\pi
as in §
3.5
.)

Using equations (
104
)–(
105
) we compute that

\displaystyle\overline{\varepsilon}_{\underline{i}-\underline{1}}\circ\gr(H_{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\overline{\varepsilon}_{-\underline{1}}\circ\underline{y}^{-\underline{i}}\circ\gr(H_{\sigma})=\gr(\mu)\circ\gr(H_{\sigma})\circ\underline{y}^{-\underline{i}}

\displaystyle=\gr(h_{\sigma})\circ\underline{y}^{-\underline{i}}=\langle\gr(x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}),-\rangle\quad\text{on $\gr(\pi^{\vee})$.}

As
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\underline{i}-\underline{1}}\circ\gr(H_{\sigma})
is a map of degree
\|\underline{i}\|
, if
(\overline{\varepsilon}_{\underline{i}-\underline{1}}\circ\gr(H_{\sigma}))(v_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{*})\neq 0
, then

\|\underline{i}\|=0
. By the definition of
x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}
and by Corollary
3.4.12
we know that
x_{\sigma,\underline{i}}=0

if
\|\underline{i}\|=0
and
\underline{i}\neq\underline{0}
. Therefore,

\gr(H_{\sigma})=\langle\gr(x_{\sigma,\underline{0}}),-\rangle\underline{y}^{-\underline{1}}=\langle\gr(v_{\sigma}),-\rangle\underline{y}^{-\underline{1}}\quad\text{on $\gr(\pi^{\vee})$,}

as desired.
∎

3.8
The
(\varphi,\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
-action on
\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)

We determine the
\varphi
- and
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
-actions on the elements
x_{\sigma}\in\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)
(
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
), as defined in §
3.7
.

We first determine the
\varphi
-action on
x_{\sigma}
. Let
v_{\sigma}\in\sigma^{N_{0}}\setminus\{0\}
be as in §
3.7
which defines
x_{\sigma}=(x_{\sigma,k})_{k\geq 0}
via (
102
).
By Lemma
3.4.3
there exists a constant
\mu_{\sigma}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
such that

v_{\delta(\sigma)}=\mu_{\sigma}\cdot\underline{Y}^{\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(v_{\sigma}),

(106)

where
\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}
is defined in (
92
).

Proposition 3.8.1
.

For any
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
we have

\varphi(x_{\sigma})=(-1)^{f-1}\mu_{\sigma}^{-1}\underline{Y}^{-\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}}x_{\delta(\sigma)}.

Proof.

Equivalently, we need to check that

x_{\delta(\sigma),k}=(-1)^{f-1}\mu_{\sigma}\underline{Y}^{\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}}(\varphi(x_{\sigma}))_{k}

(107)

for any
k\geq 0
.

We have

\underline{a_{nd+1}^{\sigma}}=\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}+p\delta(\underline{a_{nd}^{\sigma}})=\underline{a_{nd}^{\delta(\sigma)}}+p^{nd}\delta^{nd}(\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}})

(108)

by (
93
). By definition (
102
) and using (
106
), we have

\begin{array}[]{rll}x_{\delta(\sigma),k}&=&\lambda_{\delta(\sigma)}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd}^{\delta(\sigma)}}-\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd}(v_{\delta(\sigma)})\\
&=&\lambda_{\delta(\sigma)}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd}^{\delta(\sigma)}}-\underline{k}}\cdot\mu_{\sigma}\underline{Y}^{p^{nd}\delta^{nd}(\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}})}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd+1}(v_{\sigma})\\
&=&\mu_{\sigma}\lambda_{\delta(\sigma)}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd+1}^{\sigma}}-\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd+1}(v_{\sigma}),\end{array}

where we applied (
108
). On the other hand, by Lemma
3.3.5
(ii) and (
85
) the action of
\varphi
on
x_{\sigma}
can be computed on
sequences as follows: for any
k\geq 0
,

(\varphi(x_{\sigma}))_{k}=(-1)^{f-1}\underline{Y}^{\underline{p\ell}-\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(x_{\sigma,\ell}),

where
\ell
is chosen arbitrarily so that
p\ell\geq k
. Thus
we have (for
\ell
large enough)

\begin{array}[]{rll}\underline{Y}^{\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}}(\varphi(x_{\sigma}))_{k}&=&(-1)^{f-1}\underline{Y}^{\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}}\underline{Y}^{\underline{p\ell}-\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)(x_{\sigma,\ell})\\
&=&(-1)^{f-1}\underline{Y}^{\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}}\underline{Y}^{\underline{p\ell}-\underline{k}}\cdot\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\underline{Y}^{p\delta(\underline{a_{nd}^{\sigma}})-\underline{p\ell}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd+1}(v_{\sigma})\\
&=&(-1)^{f-1}\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd+1}^{\sigma}}-\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd+1}(v_{\sigma}),\end{array}

where we used again (
102
) and (
108
).
As
\lambda_{\sigma}=\lambda_{\delta(\sigma)}
by the discussion after (
96
), relation (
107
) is verified.
∎

We now determine the
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
-action on
x_{\sigma}
. By Lemma
3.3.5
(iii)
we can compute this action on the image of
x_{\sigma}
in
\Hom_{\mathbb{F}}^{\cont}(D_{A}(\pi),\mathbb{F})
(i.e. before descending).

For
a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
and
0\leq i\leq f-1
we put

f_{a,i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\frac{\overline{a}^{p^{i}}Y_{i}}{a(Y_{i})}\in 1+F_{1-p}A,

where we follow the convention in §
3.2
of just writing an index
i
instead of an index
\sigma_{i}
(in particular
f_{a,0}=f_{a,\sigma_{0}}
in (
21
)). Note that
\varphi(f_{a,i})=f_{a,i-1}^{p}
. We also let
\chi_{\sigma}:\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}\to\mathbb{F}^{\times}
denote the eigencharacter
of
\diag(-,1)
on
\sigma^{I_{1}}
.

Proposition 3.8.2
.

For any
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
and
a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
we have

a(x_{\sigma})=N_{\mathbb{F}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\overline{a})^{-1}\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})\bigg(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}f_{a,i}^{-a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}/(1-p^{d^{\prime}})}\bigg)x_{\sigma}

in
\Hom_{\mathbb{F}}^{\cont}(D_{A}(\pi),\mathbb{F})
, where
d^{\prime}=df
.

Proof.

First note that we may apply any element of
\mathbb{F}\llbracket N_{0}\rrbracket+F_{-kf-1}A
to (
102
) (with
F_{-kf-1}A
killing both
sides) by applying our convention in Remark
3.2.5
to both
x_{\sigma,k}\in\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{kf+1}]
and

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd^{\prime}}v_{\sigma}\in\pi[\mathfrak{m}_{I_{1}}^{\|\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\|+1}]
.

To simplify the notation we set
M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}f_{a,i}^{a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}/(1-p^{d^{\prime}})}
. Let us now consider
N_{\mathbb{F}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\overline{a})\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})^{-1}Ma(x_{\sigma})
.
Combining both parts of Lemma
3.2.4
and the previous paragraph, we obtain that its
k
-th component is given by the following formulas (where
\ell\gg_{k}0

and
n\gg_{\ell}0
):

\displaystyle\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})^{-1}M\frac{a(\underline{Y}^{\underline{\ell}})}{\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)x_{\sigma,\ell}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})^{-1}M\frac{a(\underline{Y}^{\underline{\ell}})}{\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}-\underline{\ell}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd^{\prime}}v_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})^{-1}M\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\frac{a(\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}})}{\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd^{\prime}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{a}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)v_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=M\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\frac{a(\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}})}{\underline{Y}^{\underline{k}}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd^{\prime}}v_{\sigma}.

Recalling that
a(Y_{i})=\overline{a}^{p^{i}}Y_{i}f_{a,i}^{-1}
and
\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}=\underline{a_{d^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}\frac{p^{nd^{\prime}}-1}{p^{d^{\prime}}-1}
the formula simplifies to

\displaystyle=M\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\bigg(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}\overline{a}^{p^{i}a_{nd^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}}\bigg)\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}-\underline{k}}\bigg(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}f_{a,i}^{-a_{nd^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}}\bigg)\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd^{\prime}}v_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=M^{p^{nd^{\prime}}}\bigg(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}\overline{a}^{p^{i}a_{nd^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}}\bigg)\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}-\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd^{\prime}}v_{\sigma}.

Now
M^{p^{nd^{\prime}}}
only matters modulo
F_{-kf-1}A
. But as
f_{a,i}\in 1+F_{-(p-1)}A
we have

M^{p^{nd^{\prime}}}\in 1+F_{-p^{nd^{\prime}}(p-1)}A
, so for
n
sufficiently large we can omit this factor.
In summary, the
k
-th component of
N_{\mathbb{F}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\overline{a})\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})^{-1}Ma(x_{\sigma})
is
given by

\big(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}\overline{a}^{p^{i}a_{nd^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}}\big)\lambda_{\sigma}^{n}\underline{Y}^{\underline{a_{nd^{\prime}}^{\sigma}}-\underline{k}}\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}{p}&{}\\
{}&{1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)^{nd^{\prime}}v_{\sigma}=\big(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}\overline{a}^{p^{i}a_{nd^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}}\big)x_{\sigma,k}.

Finally notice that
\sum p^{i}a_{nd^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}
=
(1+p^{d^{\prime}}+\cdots+p^{(n-1)d^{\prime}})\sum p^{i}a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}\equiv n\sum p^{i}a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}\pmod{q-1}
, as
f\mid d^{\prime}
.
Since
n
(sufficiently large) was arbitrary above, we deduce that
\sum p^{i}a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}\equiv 0\pmod{q-1}
, and the result follows.
∎

Let
\{x_{\sigma}^{*}:\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})\}
denote the
A
-basis of
D_{A}(\pi)
that is dual to
\{x_{\sigma}:\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})\}
.
By (
72
) we deduce the
\varphi
- and
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
-actions on the elements
x_{\sigma}^{*}
from Propositions
3.8.1
and
3.8.2
.

Corollary 3.8.3
.

Fix
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
.
We have

\varphi(x_{\sigma}^{*})=(-1)^{f-1}\mu_{\sigma}\underline{Y}^{\underline{c_{1}^{\sigma}}}x_{\delta(\sigma)}^{*},

and for
a\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
,

a(x_{\sigma}^{*})=N_{\mathbb{F}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\overline{a})\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})^{-1}\bigg(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}f_{a,i}^{a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}/(1-p^{d^{\prime}})}\bigg)x_{\sigma}^{*}.

3.9
The main theorem on
D_{A}(\pi)

We prove
D_{A}(\pi)\cong D_{A}^{\otimes}(\overline{\rho}^{\vee}(1))
which finishes the proof of Theorem
3.1.3
.

Recall that
\overline{\rho}
is as at the end of §
3.1
.

Theorem 3.9.1
.

There is an isomorphism of étale
(\varphi,{\mathcal{O}}_{K}^{\times})
-modules

D_{A}(\pi)\cong D_{A}^{\otimes}(\overline{\rho}^{\vee}(1)).

Proof.

We write
D_{A,\sigma_{0}}(\overline{\rho})=Ae_{0}\oplus Ae_{1}
with
(e_{0},e_{1})
as in Lemma
2.2.2
(for
d=2
and noting
e_{i}
instead of
1\otimes e_{i}
) when
\overline{\rho}
is absolutely irreducible and where

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cll}\varphi_{q}(e_{0})&=&\displaystyle{\lambda_{0}\Big(\frac{Y_{0}}{\varphi(Y_{0})}\Big)^{h}e_{0}}\\
\varphi_{q}(e_{1})&=&\lambda_{1}e_{1}\\
a(e_{0})&=&\displaystyle{\Big(\frac{f_{a,0}}{\varphi(f_{a,0})}\Big)^{\frac{h}{1-q}}e_{0}}\\
a(e_{1})&=&e_{1}.\end{array}\right.

(109)

when
\overline{\rho}
is (split) reducible. Let
I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\{0,1\}^{f}
and denote by
\underline{i}=(i_{j})_{j}
an element of
I
. By (
61
) and since
\varphi^{f-1-j}(e_{i_{j}})\in D_{A,{\sigma_{j+1}}}(\overline{\rho})
(see (
59
)) we have
D_{A}^{\otimes}(\overline{\rho})=\bigoplus_{\underline{i}\in I}AE_{\underline{i}}
, where

E_{\underline{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\bigotimes_{j=0}^{f-1}\varphi^{f-1-j}(e_{i_{j}}).

We will define an explicit
A
-linear isomorphism from
D_{A}^{\otimes}(\overline{\rho}^{\vee}(1))
to
D_{A}(\pi)
and check that
it is a morphism of
(\varphi,\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
-modules. Twisting
\overline{\rho}
and
\pi
by the same unramified character and using Lemma
2.9.6
and Lemma
3.1.1
, we can assume
{\det}(\overline{\rho})(p)=1
, i.e.
{\det}(\overline{\rho})=\omega_{f}^{\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}(r_{i}+1)}
. Then

D_{A}^{\otimes}(\overline{\rho}^{\vee}(1))\cong D_{A}^{\otimes}(\overline{\rho}\otimes{\det}(\overline{\rho})^{-1}\omega)=D_{A}^{\otimes}\big(\overline{\rho}\otimes\omega_{f}^{-\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}r_{i}}\big),

and using Lemma
2.9.6
and Lemma
3.1.1
again, it is equivalent to define an isomorphism
\vartheta
of
(\varphi,\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times})
-modules

D_{A}^{\otimes}(\overline{\rho})\longrightarrow D_{A}\big(\pi\otimes\omega_{f}^{-\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}r_{i}}\big)\cong D_{A}(\pi)\otimes_{A}D_{A}\big(\omega_{f}^{\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}r_{i}}\big).

(110)

We know by Theorem
3.7.1
that
\{x_{\sigma}:\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})\}
form an
A
-basis of
\Hom_{A}(D_{A}(\pi),A)
.
Let
\{x_{\sigma}^{*}:\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})\}
denote the
A
-basis of
D_{A}(\pi)
that is dual to
\{x_{\sigma}:\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})\}
, as in §
3.8
.
For convenience, below we write
x_{J}^{*}
instead of
x_{\sigma}^{*}\otimes 1
in (
110
), where
J=J_{\sigma}
.

Write
\sigma=(s_{0},\dots,s_{f-1})\otimes\eta
and
\delta(\sigma)=(s_{0}^{\prime},\dots,s_{f-1}^{\prime})\otimes\eta^{\prime}
. Below when we write, for example,
s_{i}=r_{i}+1
, we actually mean that
\lambda_{i}(x_{i})=x_{i}+1
, where
\lambda\in\mathcal{ID}(x_{0},\dots,x_{f-1})
or
\mathcal{RD}(x_{0},\dots,x_{f-1})
is the element corresponding to
\sigma
; see Remark
3.4.2
.

(i) Assume first
\overline{\rho}
is absolutely irreducible.
For
J\subseteq\{0,1,\dots,f-1\}
, with corresponding Serre weight
\sigma\in W(\overline{\rho})
, define

\vartheta:E_{\underline{i_{J}}}\longmapsto\alpha_{J}\underline{Y}^{\underline{b_{J}}-\underline{1}}x_{J}^{*},

where
\alpha_{J}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
are suitable constants,
\underline{i_{J}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\mathbf{1}_{J}
(i.e.
i_{J,j}=1
if
j\in J
and
i_{J,j}=0
if
j\notin J
), and

•

b_{J,i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}0
if either
i=0
and
s_{0}\in\{r_{0},r_{0}-1\}
, or
i>0
and
s_{i}\in\{r_{i},p-3-r_{i}\}
;

•

b_{J,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}-h^{[0]}+1
if
s_{0}=p-1-r_{0}
;

•

b_{J,i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}h^{[i]}+1
if
i>0
and
s_{i}=r_{i}+1
;

•

b_{J,i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}-h^{[i]}
if
s_{i}=p-2-r_{i}
;

where
h^{[i]}
was defined in (
94
).
Below
we check that for well-chosen
\alpha_{J}
,
\vartheta
commutes with
\varphi
, i.e.
\vartheta\big(\varphi(E_{\underline{i_{J}}})\big)=\varphi(\alpha_{J}\underline{Y}^{\underline{b_{J}}-\underline{1}}x_{J}^{*})
.
Writing
J^{\prime}=J_{\delta(\sigma)}
, Corollary
3.8.3
implies

\varphi(x_{J}^{*})=(-1)^{f-1}\mu_{J}\underline{Y}^{\underline{c_{J^{\prime}}}}x_{J^{\prime}}^{*},

(111)

where
\mu_{J}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{\tiny{{def}}}}}{{=}}\mu_{\sigma_{J}}
, and
\underline{c_{J^{\prime}}}
is defined as in §
3.4
with respect to the pair
(\sigma,\delta(\sigma))
. Also, using Lemma
2.2.2
it is easy to check that

\varphi(E_{\underline{i_{J}}})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rll}E_{\underline{i_{J^{\prime}}}}&\text{if $i_{J,0}=0,$}\\
-(\frac{Y_{\sigma_{0}}}{Y_{\sigma_{f-1}}^{p}})^{h}E_{\underline{i_{J^{\prime}}}}&\text{if $i_{J,0}=1$.}\end{array}\right.

Thus, we are reduced to checking:

•

if
i_{J,0}=0
then

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha_{J}\cdot\mu_{J}=(-1)^{f-1}\alpha_{J^{\prime}},\\
p\delta(\underline{b_{J}})+\underline{1-p}+\underline{c_{J^{\prime}}}=\underline{b_{J^{\prime}}};\end{array}\right.

(112)

•

if
i_{J,0}=1
then

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha_{J}\cdot\mu_{J}=(-1)^{f}\alpha_{J^{\prime}},\\
p\delta(\underline{b_{J}})+\underline{1-p}+\underline{c_{J^{\prime}}}=\underline{b_{J^{\prime}}}+(h,0,\dots,-ph).\end{array}\right.

(113)

First assume
0\notin J
, i.e.
s_{0}\in\{r_{0},r_{0}-1\}
; note that this implies
s_{1}\in\{r_{1},p-2-r_{1}\}
by the property of
W(\overline{\rho})
.
We need to check

pb_{J,i}+1-p+c_{J^{\prime},i-1}=b_{J^{\prime},i-1}

for any
0\leq i\leq f-1
.
It is a direct check using Lemma
3.4.1
. We do it for
i=0,1
and leave the other cases as an exercise. Recall that
c_{J^{\prime},i-1}=s_{i-1}^{\prime}
if
i-1\in J^{\rm max}(\sigma_{J})
and
c_{J^{\prime},i-1}=p-1
otherwise.

•

If
i=0
and
s_{0}=r_{0}
, then
b_{J,0}=0
by definition and
c_{J^{\prime},f-1}=s_{f-1}^{\prime}=p-2-r_{f-1}
by Lemma
3.4.1
, so we obtain

p\cdot 0+(1-p)+(p-2-r_{f-1})=-h^{[f-1]},

which is equal to
b_{J^{\prime},f-1}
.

•

If
i=0
and
s_{0}=r_{0}-1
, then
b_{J,0}=0
by definition and
c_{J^{\prime},f-1}=p-1
by Lemma
3.4.1
, so we obtain

p\cdot 0+(1-p)+(p-1)=0,

which is equal to
b_{J^{\prime},f-1}
(as
s_{f-1}^{\prime}=p-3-r_{f-1}
).

•

If
i=1
and
s_{1}=r_{1}
, then
b_{J,1}=0
by definition and
c_{J^{\prime},0}=p-1
by Lemma
3.4.1
, so we obtain

p\cdot 0+(1-p)+(p-1)=0,

which is equal to
b_{J^{\prime},0}
(as
s_{0}^{\prime}=r_{0}-1
).

•

If
i=1
and
s_{1}=p-2-r_{1}
, then
b_{J,1}=-h^{[1]}
by definition and
c_{J^{\prime},0}=s_{0}^{\prime}=p-1-r_{0}
, so we obtain

p(-h^{[1]})+(1-p)+(p-1-r_{0})=-h^{[0]}+1,

which is equal to
b_{J^{\prime},0}
.

Assume
0\in J
, i.e.
s_{0}\in\{p-2-r_{0},p-1-r_{0}\}
; note that this implies
s_{1}\in\{r_{1}+1,p-3-r_{1}\}
. We check (
113
) for
i=0
and leave the other cases as an exercise.

•

If
s_{0}=p-2-r_{0}
, then
b_{J,0}=-h^{[0]}
by definition and
c_{J^{\prime},f-1}=p-1
by Lemma
3.4.1
, so we obtain

p(-h^{[0]})+(1-p)+(p-1)=-ph,

which equals to
b_{J^{\prime},f-1}-ph
(as
b_{J^{\prime},f-1}=0
, since
s^{\prime}_{f-1}=r_{f-1}
).

•

If
s_{0}=p-1-r_{0}
, then
b_{J,0}=-h^{[0]}+1
by definition and
c_{J^{\prime},f-1}=s_{f-1}^{\prime}=r_{f-1}+1
, so we obtain

p(-h^{[0]}+1)+(1-p)+(r_{f-1}+1)=(r_{f-1}+1)+1-ph,

which is equal to
b_{J^{\prime},f-1}-ph
(as
b_{J^{\prime},f-1}=h^{[f-1]}+1
).

Now we show that the constants
\alpha_{J}
can be compatibly chosen so that
\vartheta
is
\varphi
-equivariant. Using (
112
) and (
113
) it suffices to check, for any
J
whose orbit has length
d
, that

(-1)^{d(f-1)}\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}\mu_{\delta^{j}(J)}=\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}(-1)^{i_{\delta^{j}(J),0}}.

As the left-hand side is equal to
(-1)^{-\frac{d}{2}}=(-1)^{\frac{d}{2}}
by Lemma
3.4.8
and (
97
) (and
{\det}(\overline{\rho})(p)=1
), it suffices to show that

\sharp\big\{0\leq j\leq d-1,0\in\delta^{j}(J)\big\}=\frac{d}{2}.

(114)

By the proof of
[
Bre11
, Lemma 5.2]
, letting
J^{\prime}=J\cup\{f+j,j\in\overline{J}\}
(where
\overline{J}
is the complement of
J
),
then
d
is also the smallest positive integer such that
J^{\prime}=J^{\prime}-d
as subsets of
{\mathbb{Z}}/2f{\mathbb{Z}}
, and in particular
d
divides
2f
. Since
|J^{\prime}|=f
and
J^{\prime}\cap\{0,1,\dots,f-1\}=J
, it is easy to see that

\sharp\big\{0\leq j\leq 2f-1,~0\in\delta^{j}(J)\big\}=f

from which we deduce (
114
).

We now check that
\vartheta
is
\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\times}
-equivariant. By Lemma
2.2.2
we know that

a(E_{\underline{i_{J}}})=\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}\varphi^{f-1-i}(f_{a,0}^{h(1-\varphi)q^{i_{J,i}}/(1-q^{2})})E_{\underline{i_{J}}}

and by Corollary
3.8.3
we have

a(x_{J}^{*})=N_{\mathbb{F}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\overline{a})\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})^{-1}\overline{a}^{\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}r_{i}}\bigg(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}f_{a,i}^{a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}/(1-p^{d^{\prime}})}\bigg)x_{J}^{*},

where
d^{\prime}=df
and recall the twist
D_{A}(\omega_{f}^{\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}r_{i}})
in (
110
). Thus it suffices to show that

a(\underline{Y}^{\underline{b_{J}}-\underline{1}})N_{\mathbb{F}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\overline{a})\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})^{-1}\overline{a}^{\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}r_{i}}\bigg(\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}f_{a,i}^{a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}/(1-p^{d^{\prime}})}\bigg)\\
=\prod_{i=0}^{f-1}f_{a,i+1}^{p^{f-1-i}hq^{i_{J,i}}/(1-q^{2})}f_{a,i}^{-p^{f-i}hq^{i_{J,i}}/(1-q^{2})}\underline{Y}^{\underline{b_{J}}-\underline{1}},

which is implied by the following claims (where we use that
2f\mid d^{\prime}
):

(a)

\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})=\overline{a}^{\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}b_{J,i}+\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}r_{i}}
,

(b)

\displaystyle\frac{a_{d^{\prime},i}^{\sigma}}{1-p^{d^{\prime}}}+1-b_{J,i}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{hp^{f-i}}{1-q^{2}}[q^{\mathbf{1}_{J}(i-1)}-q^{\mathbf{1}_{J}(i)}]&\text{if $1\leq i\leq f-1$,}\\
\displaystyle\frac{h}{1-q^{2}}[q^{\mathbf{1}_{J}(f-1)}-q\cdot q^{\mathbf{1}_{J}(0)}]&\text{if $i=0$}.\end{cases}

To verify the first claim, note from
[
Bre11
, §2]
that

\chi_{\sigma}(\overline{a})=\overline{a}^{\frac{1}{2}\big(\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}(r_{i}+s_{i})+(q-1)\mathbf{1}_{J}(f-1)\big)}.

It then suffices to show that

\frac{1}{2}\big(\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}(s_{i}-r_{i})+(q-1)\mathbf{1}_{J}(f-1)\big)\equiv\sum_{i=0}^{f-1}p^{i}b_{J,i}\pmod{q-1}.

First assume
f-1\notin J
(so that
\mathbf{1}_{J}(f-1)=0
), equivalently
s_{0}\in\{r_{0},p-2-r_{0}\}
. Then
(s_{0},\dots,s_{f-1})
consists of subsequences of the form
p-2-r_{j},p-3-r_{j+1},\dots,p-3-r_{j^{\prime}-1},r_{j^{\prime}}+1
for some
0\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq f-1
(and
r_{i}
for
i\notin\{j,\dots,j^{\prime}\}
). Since
b_{J,i}=0
if
s_{i}=r_{i}
, we are reduced to prove that for
0\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq f-1
,

\frac{1}{2}\big((p-2-2r_{j})+\sum_{j<i<j^{\prime}}p^{i}(p-3-2r_{i})+p^{j^{\prime}}\big)\equiv\sum_{j\leq i\leq j^{\prime}}p^{i}b_{J,i}\pmod{q-1}.

(115)

It is direct to check that the left-hand side of (
115
) is equal to

p^{j}(p-1-r_{j})+\sum_{j<i<j^{\prime}}p^{i}(p-2-r_{i})=p^{j}(p-h_{j})+\sum_{j<i<j^{\prime}}p^{i}(p-1-h_{i}).

On the other hand, by the definition of
b_{J,i}
the right-hand side of (
115
) is equal to

\begin{array}[]{rll}p^{j}(-h^{[j]})+p^{j^{\prime}}(h^{[j^{\prime}]}+1)&=&p^{j^{\prime}}-p^{j}h_{j}-\cdots p^{j^{\prime}-1}h_{j^{\prime}-1}\\
&=&p^{j}(p-h_{j})+\sum_{j<i<j^{\prime}}p^{i}(p-1-h_{i}),\end{array}

hence (
115
) is verified in this case (we actually have an equality). Now assume
f-1\in J
(so that
\mathbf{1}_{J}(f-1)=1
), equivalently
s_{0}\in\{r_{0}-1,p-1-r_{0}\}
.

\bullet

If
s_{0}=r_{0}-1
, then
(s_{0},\dots,s_{f-1})
contains a subsequence of the form
p-2-r_{j},p-3-r_{j+1},\dots,r_{0}-1
for some
0<j\leq f-1
(note that the case
j=f-1
is allowable), and one computes

\begin{array}[]{rll}&\frac{1}{2}\big(p^{j}(p-2-2r_{j})+\sum_{j<i\leq f-1}p^{i}(p-3-2r_{i})+(-1)+q-1\big)\\
=&p^{j}(p-1-r_{j})+\sum_{j<i\leq f-1}p^{i}(p-2-r_{i})+(-1)\\
\equiv&p^{j}(-h^{[j]})\pmod{q-1}.\end{array}

\bullet

If
s_{0}=p-1-r_{0}
, then
(s_{0},\dots,s_{f-1})
contains a subsequence of the form
